Generic linearized curvature singularity at the perturbed Kerr Cauchy horizon
Sebastian Gurriaran

Abstract
We prove the precise asymptotics of the spin − 2 -2 Teukolsky field in the interior and along the Cauchy horizon of a subextremal Kerr black hole. Together with the oscillatory blow-up asymptotics of the spin + 2 +2 Teukolsky field proven in our previous work [ 15 ] , our result suggests that generic perturbations of a Kerr black hole build up to form a coordinate-independent curvature singularity at the Cauchy horizon. This supports the Strong Cosmic Censorship conjecture in Kerr spacetimes. Unlike in the spin + 2 +2 case, the spin − 2 -2 Teukolsky field is regular on the Cauchy horizon and the first term in its asymptotic development vanishes. As a result, the derivation of a precise lower bound for the spin − 2 -2 field is more delicate than in the spin + 2 +2 case, and relies on a novel ODE method based on a decomposition of the Teukolsky operator between radial and time derivatives.



1
Introduction

1.1
Kerr black holes and Strong Cosmic Censorship

1.1.1
The Kerr black hole interior

The Kerr metric describes a spacetime around and inside a non-charged rotating black hole. Its expression in Boyer-Lindquist coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\phi)
is

\mathbf{g}_{a,M}=-\frac{(\Delta-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta)}{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}t^{2}-\frac{4aMr}{\Sigma}\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}\phi+\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\Sigma\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\Delta}{\Sigma}\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}\>,

where
M>0
and
aM
are respectively the mass and the angular momentum of the black hole, and where

\Delta:=r^{2}-2rM+a^{2},\quad\Sigma:=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta.

The main features of the Kerr metric is that it is a 2-parameter family of stationary, axisymmetric, and asymptotically flat solutions of the Einstein vacuum equation of general relativity that writes

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}[\mathbf{g}]=0,

(1.1)

where
\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}[\mathbf{g}]
is the Ricci tensor of the Lorentzian metric
\mathbf{g}
.

In the context of this work, we will only consider subextremal Kerr black holes with non-zero angular momentum, i.e. such that

0<|a|<M
. In this setting,
\Delta=(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-})
has two distincts real roots

r_{\pm}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}},\quad 0<r_{-}<r_{+}.

The sets
\{r=r_{\pm}\}
are in Boyer-Lindquist coordinate degeneracies. Defining instead Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates

u=r^{*}+t,\quad{\underline{u}}=r^{*}-t,\quad\phi_{\pm}=\phi\pm r_{mod},

where
\mathrm{d}r^{*}/\mathrm{d}r=(r^{2}+a^{2})/\Delta
,
\mathrm{d}r_{mod}/\mathrm{d}r=a/\Delta
, one can prove
1
1

1

See for example
[
29
, Chapt. 2]
.
that the hypersurfaces

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{+}:=\{{\underline{u}}=-\infty\},\quad\mathcal{H_{+}}:=\{u=-\infty\},\quad\mathcal{CH_{+}}:=\{{\underline{u}}=+\infty\},\quad\mathcal{CH}^{\prime}_{+}:=\{u=+\infty\}

can be properly attached to the Lorentzian manifold

(\mathbb{R}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}_{\underline{u}}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\mathbf{g}_{a,M})\simeq((r_{-},r_{+})\times\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\mathbf{g}_{a,M}).

We call the resulting manifold the Kerr black hole interior, that admits as boundaries the event horizon of the black hole

\mathcal{H_{+}}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{+}=\{u=-\infty\}\cup\{{\underline{u}}=+\infty\}=\{r=r_{+}\},

and the Cauchy horizon

\mathcal{CH_{+}}\cup\mathcal{CH}^{\prime}_{+}=\{u=+\infty\}\cup\{{\underline{u}}=+\infty\}=\{r=r_{-}\}.

1.1.2
Strong Cosmic Censorship conjecture

The Kerr spacetime is globally hyperbolic only up to the Cauchy horizon. Indeed, it admits infinitely many extensions across the Cauchy horizon as a smooth solution of the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
), see Figure
1
. This gives rise to a failure of determinism as in that case, the data on any complete spacelike hypersurface inside an exact Kerr black hole does not determine its future development.

Figure 1:
The Penrose diagram of Kerr spacetime, with the exterior of the black hole in white, the interior of the black hole in the darker shaded grey region, and the region with infinitely many extensions of Kerr in the lightest shaded grey region.

It is expected that this behavior is an artifact of exact Kerr spacetimes, and vanishes upon small perturbations. This is the statement of the Strong Cosmic Censorship (SCC) conjecture in the case of Kerr. First formulated by Penrose
[
33
]
2
2

2

For more modern versions of the Strong Cosmic Censorship conjecture, see
[
3
,
4
]
.
, the SCC conjecture roughly states that the maximal globally hyperbolic development of generic initial data for the Einstein equations is inextendible as a
regular
Lorentzian manifold. In other words, it states that the non-deterministic behavior of spacetimes with non-empty Cauchy horizons, like Kerr and Reissner-Nordström, is non-generic and vanishes upon small perturbations. Note that the
C^{0}
version of SCC was disproved in Kerr by Dafermos and Luk
[
8
]
. In fact, they prove the
C^{0}
stability of the Cauchy horizon and the proof suggests that the perturbed Cauchy horizon should be a weak null singularity as in
[
18
]
.

The results of the present paper
3
3

3

Namely, the precise asymptotics of the spin
-2
Teukolsky field near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
.
, together with the results of our previous work
[
15
]
, suggest a precise and coordinate-invariant linearized version of this expected instability of the Kerr Cauchy horizon, see the discussion in Section
1.3
for more details.

1.2
Teukolsky equations

In this work, we consider a linearized gravitational perturbation of a Kerr black hole. A classical result by Teukolsky
[
39
]
states that two linearized curvature components decouple from the linearized gravity system in the Newman-Penrose formalism and satisfy wave equations called the
Teukolsky equations
.

To write the Teukolsky equations, we first define the pair of null vector fields

\displaystyle n:=\left[(r^{2}+a^{2})\partial_{t}-\Delta\partial_{r}+a\partial_{\phi}\right]/(2\Sigma),\quad l:=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\partial_{t}+\partial_{r}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\partial_{\phi},

(1.2)

which are aligned with the principal null directions of Kerr, and the complex vector field

\displaystyle m:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(r+ia\cos\theta)}\left(ia\sin\theta\partial_{t}+\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\phi}\right).

(1.3)

They satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(l,n)=-1,\quad\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(m,\overline{m})=1,

(1.4)

and all other scalar products are zero. Then, denoting by
\dot{\mathbf{W}}
the linearized Weyl tensor, Teukolsky discovered that the scalars

\widehat{\psi}_{+2}:=\dot{\mathbf{W}}_{lmlm},\quad\widehat{\psi}_{-2}:=(r-ia\cos\theta)^{4}\dot{\mathbf{W}}_{n\overline{m}n\overline{m}},

satisfy the following wave equations, for
s=\pm 2
:

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{\left(r^{2}+a^{2}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right]\partial_{t}^{2}\widehat{\psi}_{s}-\frac{4Mar}{\Delta}\partial_{t}\partial_{\phi}\widehat{\psi}_{s}-\left[\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right]\partial_{\phi}^{2}\widehat{\psi}_{s}

\displaystyle+\Delta^{-s}\partial_{r}\left(\Delta^{s+1}\partial_{r}\widehat{\psi}_{s}\right)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}\left(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi}_{s}\right)+2s\left[\frac{a(r-M)}{\Delta}+\frac{i\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right]\partial_{\phi}\widehat{\psi}_{s}

\displaystyle+2s\left[\frac{M\left(r^{2}-a^{2}\right)}{\Delta}-r-ia\cos\theta\right]\partial_{t}\widehat{\psi}_{s}-\left[\frac{s^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}-s\right]\widehat{\psi}_{s}=0.

(1.5)

Note that
n
and
l
are regular on
4
4

4

This can be seen using outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates.

\mathcal{CH_{+}}
, thus
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
and
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
are projections of the linearized curvature tensor on a tetrad
(n,l,m,\overline{m})
which is regular on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
. We also define the rescaled scalars

\psi_{+2}:=\Delta^{2}\widehat{\psi}_{+2},\quad\psi_{-2}:=\Delta^{-2}\widehat{\psi}_{-2},

which are projections of the linearized Weyl tensor on the tetrad
(\Delta^{-1}n,\Delta l,m,\overline{m})
that is regular on
\mathcal{H_{+}}
. These rescaled scalars satisfy a rescaled version of the Teukolsky equation, see Section
2.5
.

Note that in
[
17
]
, the proof of the non-linear stability of the exterior of slowly rotating Kerr black holes relied on decay estimates for solutions of the Teukolsky equations in the non-linear setting proven in
[
13
]
. For a review of the literature concerning the Teukolsky equations, see the introduction in
[
13
]
. In the present work, we will use the Teukolsky equations to prove a precise quantitative and coordinate-independent curvature blow-up on the perturbed Kerr Cauchy horizon, in the linearized setting.

1.3
Coordinate-independent curvature singularity

As mentioned in Section
1.1.2
, the Kerr Cauchy horizon was proven to be
C^{0}
stable
5
5

5

More precisely, it was proven that small perturbations of the Kerr interior have a future null boundary (the perturbed Cauchy horizon) in a neighborhood of timelike infinity, across which the metric extends continuously. Moreover the perturbed metric is
C^{0}
-close to the Kerr metric.
in
[
8
]
. Thus, the expected instability of the Kerr Cauchy horizon must be at a higher regularity level. In the recent paper by Sbierski
[
38
]
, it is shown that if one manages to prove, in the non-linear setting, the blow-up of the curvature tensor contracted with small perturbations of some continuous fixed vector fields
6
6

6

These contractions of the curvature tensor should be thought as perturbations of
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
in the non-linear setting.
, integrated on a spacetime region approaching the perturbed Cauchy horizon, then the spacetime is
C^{0,1}_{loc}
-inextendible across the Cauchy horizon. This result suggests that a proof of the
C^{0,1}_{loc}
version of SCC conjecture in Kerr spacetimes could be obtained by proving the generic (integrated) blow-up of the non-linear analog of the spin
+2
Teukolsky scalar (which is a projection of the curvature on some fixed vector fields, and where the blow-up would have to hold when projecting the curvature tensor on perturbations of these vector fields).

In this section, we outline a different strategy to address the instability of the Kerr Cauchy horizon, by obtaining a generic coordinate-invariant curvature blow-up result at the perturbed Cauchy horizon, from the precise control of both spin
\pm 2
Teukolsky scalars.

The most classical way to detect coordinate-invariant singularities in general relativity is by proving blow-up of the Kretschmann scalar
7
7

7

The most famous example is the blow-up of the Kretschmann scalar of Schwarzschild spacetime at
r=0
, proving that the singularity
\{r=0\}
of Schwarzschild is a true singularity.
, which is the coordinate-independent curvature scalar

\mathbf{K}:=\mathbf{R}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\mathbf{R}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta},

where
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
is the curvature tensor of
\mathbf{g}
. One can express
\mathbf{K}
as projections of the curvature tensor on a null frame, and this expression is by definition
frame independent
.

Let
(n,l,m,\overline{m})
be any null tetrad satisfying (
1.4
). Then defining the Weyl scalars

\displaystyle\widehat{\Psi}_{-2}:=\mathbf{W}_{n\overline{m}n\overline{m}},\quad\widehat{\Psi}_{-1}:=\mathbf{W}_{ln\overline{m}n},\quad\widehat{\Psi}_{0}:=\mathbf{W}_{lm\overline{m}n},\quad\widehat{\Psi}_{+1}:=\mathbf{W}_{lnlm},\quad\widehat{\Psi}_{+2}:=\mathbf{W}_{lmlm},

where
\mathbf{W}
is the Weyl tensor, we have in a vacuum spacetime
8
8

8

Note that if the metric solves the Einstein vacuum equation (
1.1
), then
\mathbf{R}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}=\mathbf{W}_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}
.

\mathbf{K}=16\mathfrak{R}(\widehat{\Psi}_{+2}\widehat{\Psi}_{-2}+3\widehat{\Psi}_{0}^{2}-4\widehat{\Psi}_{+1}\widehat{\Psi}_{-1}).

Moreover, we expect that in generic perturbations of the Kerr black hole interior, for a frame
(n,l,m,\overline{m})
which is a perturbation of (
1.2
)-(
1.3
), the curvature terms
\widehat{\Psi}_{0}^{2}
and
\widehat{\Psi}_{+1}\widehat{\Psi}_{-1}
are negligible
9
9

9

See
[
8
]
for estimates supporting this claim in the non-linear setting, and
[
32
]
for a discussion in the linearized setting.
compared to
\widehat{\Psi}_{+2}\widehat{\Psi}_{-2}
near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
so that we have

\mathbf{K}=16\mathfrak{R}(\widehat{\Psi}_{+2}\widehat{\Psi}_{-2}(1+o(1)))

near the Cauchy horizon. In the linearized setting, we have by definition
\dot{\widehat{\Psi}}_{+2}=\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
,
\dot{\widehat{\Psi}}_{-2}=(r-ia\cos\theta)^{-4}\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
.
The product
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
should thus be thought as the leading-order term of the linearized Kretschmann curvature invariant.
Although we know from
[
15
]
that in the gauge (
1.2
),
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
generically blows-up at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
while violently oscillating, this discussion highlights the importance of proving in addition that
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
generically does not vanish on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
. This fact is a direct consequence of the main result of this work, which implies that the product
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
generically blows-up at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
while oscillating violently, see (
1.8
).

In conclusion, the linearized analysis of
[
15
]
for
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
and of the present paper for
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
thus supports the conjecture that generic perturbations of Kerr black holes present a Kretschmann curvature singularity on the perturbed Cauchy horizon, see
[
32
,
2
]
for corresponding heuristics and numerical results. We give more details on the expected oscillating behavior of the curvature blow-up at the end of Section
1.4
.

1.4
Rough version of the main theorem and consequences

We now state the simplified version of our main result, see Theorem
3.3
for the precise formulation.

Theorem 1.1

(Main theorem, rough version)
.

We consider an initially smooth and compactly supported linearized gravitational perturbation of a Kerr black hole such that
0<|a|<M
. We denote
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
the spin
-2
Teukolsky scalar obtained in a principal null frame regular at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
. Then
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
is regular on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
, and satisfies the following asymptotic behavior near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{-2}\sim\frac{1}{u^{8}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}C_{m}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}},

(1.6)

where the constants
C_{m}
are defined in (
3.6
), depend only on the initial data and black hole parameters and are generically non-zero, and the functions
Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}
are the spin
-2
spherical harmonics which are regular on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
(see Sections
2.3
and
3
for more details).

Remark 1.2
.

A few remarks are in order:

1.

At first glance, the regularity of
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
may seem to support a curvature stability statement for the Kerr Cauchy horizon. But the asymptotic behavior (
1.6
) implies in particular that
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
is generically non-zero near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}\cap\{u\ll-1\}
. As discussed in Section
1.3
, together with the blow-up asymptotics of
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
proven in
[
15
]
, this suggests the presence of a coordinate-independent curvature singularity at the Cauchy horizon of a generically perturbed Kerr black hole.

2.

From Price’s law results in
[
25
]
,
[
28
]
, which predict a generic asymptotic behavior in
1/{\underline{u}}^{7}
on the event horizon for
\psi_{-2}
, and the analysis in
[
15
]
for
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
, one could expect that the generic asymptotic behavior for
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
in the black hole interior would be in
1/u^{7}
. But it turns out that, unlike for the spin
+2
case
[
15
]
, the first term in the asymptotic development of
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
in inverse powers of
u
vanishes at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
. The crucial part of the proof of Theorem
1.1
is thus to track the next term in the asymptotic development of
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
, and to prove that it is generically non-zero at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
. Note that the asymptotic behavior
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}\sim 1/u^{8}
was first heuristically predicted by Ori
[
30
]
.

3.

The proof is based on the energy method introduced in
[
24
]
and on a novel ODE method that relies on a decomposition of the Teukolsky operator between radial and time derivatives. For more details and for a comparison with the method used for
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
, see Section
1.6
.

4.

Just like in the spin
+2
case in
[
15
]
, we do not assume higher order decay of the higher modes
(\psi_{-2})_{\ell\geq 3}
on the event horizon.

We now complete the discussion of Section
1.3
by analyzing the strength of the curvature singularity on the Cauchy horizon suggested by the asymptotic behaviors of
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
and
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
. The main result of
[
15
]
yields

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{+2}\sim\frac{\Delta^{-2}(u,{\underline{u}})}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}A_{m}(r_{-})Q_{m,2}e^{2imr_{mod}}Y_{m,2}^{+2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}

(1.7)

near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
, where
r_{mod}
,
Q_{m,2}
,
A_{m}(r)
are defined in
[
15
]
, and satisfy
r_{mod}\sim a\log(r-r_{-})
near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
,
A_{m}(r_{-})\neq 0
for
m=\pm 1,\pm 2
and the constants
Q_{m,2}
depend on the initial data and are generically non-zero. Using (
1.7
) and (
1.6
) we get

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{+2}\widehat{\psi}_{-2}\sim\frac{\Delta^{-2}(u,{\underline{u}})}{{\underline{u}}^{7}u^{8}}\left(\sum_{|m|\leq 2}A_{m}(r_{-})Q_{m,2}e^{2imr_{mod}}Y_{m,2}^{+2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}\right)\left(\sum_{|m|\leq 2}C_{m}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}\right),

(1.8)

near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
, which generically blows-up at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
(because
\Delta^{-2}\sim e^{\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2Mr_{-}}{\underline{u}}}
on
\{u=cst\}
) while oscillating violently. In view of the discussion in Section
1.3
, this supports the following predictions by Ori
[
32
]
:

1.

Generic perturbations of a Kerr black hole have a Kretschmann scalar which is singular on the perturbed Cauchy horizon.

2.

On every slice
\{u=u_{0}\}
for sufficiently negative values of
u_{0}\ll-1
, the generic curvature blow-up profile is a violently oscillating exponential factor in
{\underline{u}}
divided by a polynomial in
{\underline{u}}
.

1.5
Perturbations of black hole interiors

1.5.1
Price’s law-type results

To prove the precise asymptotic behavior of solutions of the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation in the Kerr black hole interior, the starting point is a precise version of Price’s law for Teukolsky.
This law gives polynomial upper and lower bounds on the event horizon for solutions of wave equations arising from compactly supported initial data, see the original works on Price’s law
[
14
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
. A version of Price’s law for Teukolsky equations in Kerr was heuristically found by Barack and Ori in
[
1
]
.

In this paper we use the Price’s law-type precise asymptotics of the spin
-2
Teukolsky field on
\mathcal{H}_{+}
proven by Ma and Zhang
[
25
]
10
10

10

The Price’s law in
[
25
]
holds for
|a|\ll M
, and for
|a|<M
conditionally on an energy-Morawetz bound. This energy-Morawetz estimate has since been proved for
|a|<M
by Teixeira da Costa and Shlapentokh-Rothman in
[
5
,
6
]
, so that the Price’s law in
[
25
]
holds for the full subextremal range
|a|<M
.
. For a proof of the precise asymptotics of solutions to the Teukolsky equations in the full subextremal range
|a|<M
with a spectral point of view, see
[
28
]
. For a complete review of Price’s law-type results, see the introduction in
[
25
]
.

1.5.2
Previous results on the interior of perturbed black holes

The first studies of linear perturbations of Kerr and Reissner-Nordström black holes interiors explicited specific perturbations that become unbounded in some way at the Cauchy horizon, see for example
[
26
]
. A heuristic power law for scalar waves in Kerr spacetimes was obtained by Ori in
[
31
]
. For the Teukolsky equations, the oscillatory blow-up asymptotic of the spin
+2
Teukolsky scalar in the interior of Kerr black holes was first predicted heuristically by Ori
[
30
]
. In the same paper, the asymptotic behavior
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}\sim 1/u^{8}
for the spin
-2
Teukolsky scalar was also predicted, writing the azimuthal
m
-mode of the solution as a late-time expansion ansatz.

Next, inside Reissner-Nordström black holes, a rigorous boundedness statement for solutions of the scalar wave equation was proven in
[
11
]
. For a scattering approach to Cauchy horizon instability in Reissner-Nordström, as well as an application to mass inflation, see
[
22
]
. The blow-up of the energy of generic scalar waves inside Reissner-Nordström black holes was obtained in
[
19
]
. The non-linear problem of Cauchy horizon instability in spherical symmetry was treated in
[
7
]
, with results later extended in
[
20
,
21
]
. Analog results for the Einstein-Maxwell-Klein-Gordon system were proven in
[
40
,
41
]
, see also
[
42
]
.

Now we presents results for the scalar wave in the interior of Kerr black holes. A generic blow-up result on the Cauchy horizon for the energy of scalar waves was obtained in
[
23
]
. It was proven in
[
16
]
in the slowly rotating case that scalar waves remain bounded up to the Cauchy horizon. This result was then extended to the full subextremal range in
[
10
]
. The paper
[
9
]
constructs solutions that remain bounded but have infinite energy at the Cauchy horizon. More recently,
[
24
]
introduced a robust physical-space method to obtain the precise asymptotics of the scalar field in the interior of Kerr.

Concerning the Teukolsky equations in the Kerr black hole interior, the method of proof of
[
23
]
was extended to the spin
+2
Teukolsky equation in
[
37
]
, where the generic blow-up of a weighted
L^{2}
norm on a hypersurface transverse to the Cauchy horizon was proven, relying on frequency analysis. The precise pointwise oscillatory blow-up asymptotic behavior of the spin
+2
Teukolsky scalar was proven by the author in
[
15
]
, extending the method of proof of
[
24
]
.

For a more complete account of the results related to black hole interior perturbations, for example in the cosmological setting or in Schwarzschild spacetime, we refer to the introduction in
[
37
]
.

1.6
Structure of the proof

The proof of (
1.6
) is based on energy estimates, on a precise decomposition of the Teukolsky operator, and on the following Price’s law-type result on the event horizon:

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}^{j}\left(\psi_{-2}-\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)\right|\lesssim{\underline{u}}^{-7-j-\delta}

(1.9)

on
\mathcal{H_{+}}\cap\{{\underline{u}}\geq 1\}
, where the constants
Q_{m,2}
depend on the initial data, and where the functions
Y^{-2}_{m,2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}
are the spin
-2
spherical harmonics. It was proven in
[
25
]
that (
1.9
) holds for initially smooth and compactly supported solutions of the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation in Kerr spacetime. On top of (
1.9
), fixing
r_{\mathfrak{b}}\in(r_{-},r_{+})
close to
r_{-}
and
\gamma>0
small, we will use a decomposition of the Kerr black hole interior in different regions:

•

The redshift region
\mathbf{I}=\{r_{\mathfrak{b}}\leq r\leq r_{+}\}\cap\{{\underline{u}}\geq 1\}
.

•

The blueshift region
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}=\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\}
, where
w=u-r+r_{-}
,

\mathbf{II}=\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{2r^{*}\leq{\underline{u}}^{\gamma}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\},\quad\mathbf{III}=\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{2r^{*}\geq{\underline{u}}^{\gamma}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\},

and where
w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}=2r_{\mathfrak{b}}^{*}-(2r_{\mathfrak{b}}^{*})^{1/\gamma}-r_{\mathfrak{b}}+r_{-}
, see Figure
4
which illustrates regions
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{II},\mathbf{III}
.

The proof is then structured as follows:

1.

First, in Proposition
3.2
we use the redshift energy estimates results already proven in
[
15
]
for the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation in redshift region
\mathbf{I}
, to propagate the decay (
1.9
) from
\mathcal{H_{+}}
to the whole region
\mathbf{I}
.

2.

Next, in Section
4
we implement an energy method for the rescaled spin
-2
Teukolsky operator in the blueshift region
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
. Combining the symmetries of Kerr spacetime and these energy estimates yields pointwise upper bound results for the spin
-2
Teukolsky field near the Kerr Cauchy horizon.

3.

Using this energy method, in Propositions
4.11
and
4.21
we obtain a bound of the type, for
j\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}^{j}\left(\widehat{\psi}_{-2}-\frac{\Delta^{2}}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)\right|\lesssim|u|^{-7-j-\delta},

(1.10)

in the blueshift region
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
. Like in
[
15
]
, this relies on the following key computational fact:

\mathbf{T}_{-2}\left(\psi_{-2}-\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)=-\mathbf{T}_{-2}\left(\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)=O({\underline{u}}^{-8}),

where
\mathbf{T}_{-2}
is the spin
-2
Teukolsky operator. We also use in particular the fact that the scalar
-\Delta
decays exponentially in
{\underline{u}}
in region
\mathbf{III}
. The bound (
1.10
) is precise in any region with
r
bounded away from
r_{-}
, but is imprecise near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
(as
\Delta^{2}/{\underline{u}}^{7}
becomes negligible compared to
|u|^{-7-\delta}
and vanishes on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
).

4.

The crux of the proof of the main theorem is to then re-inject the bounds (
1.9
) in
\mathbf{I}
, and (
1.10
) in
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
, in the Teukolsky equation to recover a lower bound for
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
which is precise at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
. We do this in Section
5
in the following way: we first rewrite the Teukolsky equation in
\mathbf{I}
as

\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{r}]}\psi_{-2}+\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{t}]}\psi_{-2}=0,

where
\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{t}]}
is the part of the Teukolsky operator which contains
\partial_{t}
derivatives, and
\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{r}]}
is the remaining part, see Section
2.5.2
for the precise expressions of
\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{r}]},\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{t}]}
. We then define

\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]:=\psi_{-2}-\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}.

It can be checked using (
1.9
) (which holds in
\mathbf{I}
by Step
1
) that the Teukolsky equation implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{r}]}\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]=-\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{t}]}\left(\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)+O({\underline{u}}^{-8-\delta}),\quad\text{ in }\mathbf{I}.

(1.11)

The first term on the right-hand-side of (
1.11
) is a source term which can be precisely computed and which behaves like
1/{\underline{u}}^{8}
. It turns out that by using the precise expression of the operator
\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{r}]}
, when projecting (
1.11
) on a spin weighted
(\ell,m)
mode, we obtain an ODE in
r
for the
(\ell,m)
component of
\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]
, with a precise source term in
1/{\underline{u}}^{8}
. This ODE can then be integrated to obtain a precise expression for
(\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}])_{\ell,m}
in
\mathbf{I}
. Then, we do a similar procedure in region
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
for the quantity
\mathrm{Err}[\widehat{\psi}_{-2}]=\Delta^{2}\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]
using (
1.10
), and matching the solutions of the ODEs on the boundary
\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}
between
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
, we obtain a precise expression in
1/u^{8}
for
(\mathrm{Err}[\widehat{\psi}_{-2}])_{\ell=2}
(and thus also for
(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell=2}
) near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
, and the following precise upper bound:

|(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell\geq 3}|\lesssim|u|^{-8-\delta/2}+r-r_{-}.

Note that for technical reasons, we deal with the
\ell=3
mode and each
\ell\geq 4
mode of
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
separately. Moreover, the summation of the bounds obtained for each
\ell\geq 4
mode must be done with care, see the first item of Remark
5.15
.

We conclude this section by outlining the difference of treatments between
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
in this paper and
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
in
[
15
]
. It was proven by the author in
[
15
]
that the quantities

\displaystyle\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{+2}]:=\psi_{+2}-\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}A_{m}(r)Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{+2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}},

are bounded by
{\underline{u}}^{-7-\delta}
on
\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{{\underline{u}}\geq 1\}
. Roughly stated, the energy method adapted from
[
24
]
yields

|\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{+2}]|\lesssim{\underline{u}}^{-7-\delta},\quad|\Delta^{2}\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]|\lesssim|u|^{-7-\delta},

in
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
(see
[
15
]
for the first bound and Section
4
for the second bound) which implies in
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
, after renormalization:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{+2}=\frac{\Delta^{-2}(u,{\underline{u}})}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}A_{m}(r_{-})Q_{m,2}e^{2imr_{mod}}Y_{m,2}^{+2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}+O(\Delta^{-2}{\underline{u}}^{-7-\delta}),

(1.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{-2}=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}+O(|u|^{-7-\delta}).

(1.13)

Statement (
1.12
) is the main theorem of
[
15
]
and implies oscillatory blow-up of
\widehat{\psi}_{+2}
at
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
, but on the other hand we see that (
1.13
) is not precise enough, as near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
the error term
O(|u|^{-7-\delta})
becomes the leading-order term in (
1.13
). This is why we need a much finer analysis based on the ODE method in Section
5
(outlined in Step
4
above), to prove the precise
1/u^{8}
asymptotics of this error term. Moreover, unlike in the spin
+2
case in
[
15
]
where there was no need for mode projection, in the present paper we need to track the decay of the different angular modes of
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
. More precisely, we have to provide a separate analysis for the modes
\ell=2
,
\ell=3
, and
\ell\geq 4
of
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
.

1.7
Overview of the paper

In Section
2
, we introduce the Kerr black hole interior geometry, as well as some preliminary results. Next, in Section
3
, we state the assumptions and the precise version of the main theorem of the paper, Theorem
3.3
. We begin the proof of Theorem
3.3
in Section
4
by implementing the energy method for the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
. Finally, in Section
5
, we find the precise asymptotics of
(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell=2}
, and precise upper bounds for
(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell=3}
and
(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell\geq 4}
near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
.

1.8
Acknowledgments

The author would like to thank Jérémie Szeftel for helpful suggestions and for encouraging him to work on this problem. The author is partially supported by ERC-2023 AdG 101141855 BlaHSt.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notations

We begin by introducing some notations.

•

Throughout the paper, ‘RHS’ and ‘LHS’ refer respectively to ‘right-hand side’ and ‘left-hand side’.

•

If
L
is an operator acting on a spin-weighted scalar
\psi
, and if
N
is any norm, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
we use the convention

N(L^{\leq k}\psi):=\sum_{i=0}^{k}N(L^{i}\psi),

so that having a bound for
N(L^{\leq k}\psi)
is equivalent
11
11

11

In this paper,
k
will be chosen such that
k\leq k_{0}
for some fixed large enough
k_{0}
.
to having a bound for
N(L^{i}\psi)
, for all
0\leq i\leq k
.

•

If
f
and
g
are two non-negative scalars, we write
f\lesssim g
if there is a constant
C>0
which depends only on the black hole parameters
a,M
, on the initial data, and on the constants
r_{\mathfrak{b}}
,
\gamma
, such that
f\leq Cg
in the region considered. We write
f=O(g)
when
|f|\lesssim|g|
. We write
f\sim g
when
f\lesssim g
and
g\lesssim f
.

2.2
Geometric preliminaries

2.2.1
Vector fields

We begin by defining a rescaled and more convenient version of the null pair
(n,l)
defined in (
1.2
):

\displaystyle e_{3}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\partial_{r}+\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\partial_{t}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\partial_{\phi}\right),\quad\quad e_{4}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{r}+\partial_{t}+\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\partial_{\phi}\right).

This pair satisfies

\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(e_{3},e_{3})=\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(e_{4},e_{4})=0,\quad\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(e_{3},e_{4})=-\frac{\Sigma}{2(r^{2}+a^{2})},

and is regular on
\mathcal{H}_{+}
, as can be seen by expressing
e_{3}
and
e_{4}
with respect to the ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinate vector fields, see Section
2.2.2
. Let
\mu
be the scalar function

\mu:=\frac{\Delta}{r^{2}+a^{2}}

where
\Delta=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}=(r-r_{+})(r-r_{-})
. Notice that both
\mu
and
\Delta
are non-positive in the Kerr black hole interior and vanish only on the event and Cauchy horizons. We now define the following rescaled null pair, which is regular on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
:

\widehat{e_{3}}:=(-\mu)e_{3},\quad\quad\widehat{e_{4}}:=(-\mu)^{-1}e_{4}.

For convenience, we introduce the following notations for the Kerr Killing vector fields, expressed in Boyer-Lindquist coordinates,

T:=\partial_{t},\quad\quad\Phi:=\partial_{\phi}.

2.2.2
Scalar functions and coordinates

The Boyer-Lindquist (B-L) coordinates are singular at both the event and Cauchy horizons. In this paper, we will use both the Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates and double null-like coordinates
(u,{\underline{u}},\theta,\phi_{\pm})
. The classical definition of the tortoise coordinate
r^{*}
in the Kerr black hole interior is:

r^{*}(r)=\int_{M}^{r}\mu^{-1}(r^{\prime})\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}.

We have
r^{*}\to-\infty
as
r\to r_{+}
and
r^{*}\to+\infty
as
r\to r_{-}
. Indeed, defining
\kappa_{+}
and
\kappa_{-}
the surface gravities of the event and Cauchy horizons:

\kappa_{+}:=\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{4Mr_{+}}>0,\quad\kappa_{-}:=\frac{r_{-}-r_{+}}{4Mr_{-}}<0,

then the asymptotics of
r^{*}
at the horizons are given by

\displaystyle r^{*}=\frac{1}{2\kappa_{+}}\ln(r_{+}-r)+h_{+}(r)=\frac{1}{2\kappa_{-}}\ln(r-r_{-})+h_{-}(r),

(2.1)

where
h_{\pm}(r)
has a finite limit as
r\to r_{\pm}
. Notice that (
2.1
) implies that, for
r
close to
r_{+}
,

-\Delta\sim\exp(-2\kappa_{+}|r^{*}|),

while for
r
close to
r_{-}
,

-\Delta\sim\exp(-2|\kappa_{-}|r^{*}).

Next, we define the coordinates

{\underline{u}}:=r^{*}+t,\quad u:=r^{*}-t.

The range of the coordinates
u,{\underline{u}},r^{*}
is indicated on Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
The range of the coordinates
u,{\underline{u}},r^{*}
in the Kerr black hole interior.

As in
[
24
]
, we define the function
r_{mod}
by

r_{mod}(r)=\int_{M}^{r}\frac{a}{\Delta(r^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}.

We then define the ingoing and outgoing angular coordinates

\phi_{+}:=\phi+r_{mod}\>\>\text{mod}\>2\pi,\quad\phi_{-}:=\phi-r_{mod}\>\>\text{mod}\>2\pi.

The coordinates
{\underline{u}}
and
\phi_{+}
are regular on
\mathcal{H}_{+}=\{u=-\infty\}
and
\mathcal{CH}^{\prime}_{+}=\{u=+\infty\}
, while
u
and
\phi_{-}
are regular on
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{+}=\{{\underline{u}}=-\infty\}
and
\mathcal{CH_{+}}=\{{\underline{u}}=+\infty\}
.

Ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates.
The ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates are
({\underline{u}}_{\mathrm{in}}={\underline{u}},r_{\mathrm{in}}=r,\theta_{\mathrm{in}}=\theta,\phi_{\mathrm{in}}=\phi_{+})
. It is a set of coordinates regular on
\mathcal{H_{+}}
and
\mathcal{CH}^{\prime}_{+}
. The coordinate vector fields are

\displaystyle\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}=-2e_{3},\quad\partial_{{\underline{u}}_{\mathrm{in}}}=T,\quad\partial_{\theta_{\mathrm{in}}}=\partial_{\theta},\quad\partial_{\phi_{\mathrm{in}}}=\Phi.

(2.2)

Outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates.
The outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates are
(u_{\mathrm{out}}=u,r_{\mathrm{out}}=r,\theta_{\mathrm{out}}=\theta,\phi_{\mathrm{out}}=\phi_{-})
. It is a set of coordinates regular on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{+}
. The coordinate vector fields are

\displaystyle\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}=-2\widehat{e_{4}},\quad\partial_{u_{\mathrm{out}}}=T,\quad\partial_{\theta_{\mathrm{out}}}=\partial_{\theta},\quad\partial_{\phi_{\mathrm{out}}}=\Phi.

(2.3)

Double null-like coordinate systems.
We use the ingoing double null-like coordinates
({\underline{u}},u,\theta,\phi_{+})
, as in
[
24
]
. The coordinate vector fields are respectively

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{u}}=e_{4}-\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi,\quad\partial_{u}=-\mu e_{3},\quad\partial_{\theta},\quad\partial_{\phi_{+}}=\Phi.

(2.4)

The equivalent outgoing double null-like coordinates are
({\underline{u}},u,\theta,\phi_{-})
, with coordinate vector fields

\displaystyle\partial_{\underline{u}}=e_{4},\quad\partial_{u}=-\mu e_{3}+\frac{a}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi,\quad\partial_{\theta},\quad\partial_{\phi_{-}}=\Phi.

(2.5)

We will only use the ingoing double null-like coordinate system in region
\mathbf{I}
, and the outgoing double null-like coordinate system in region
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
so we use the same notations for the ingoing and outgoing double null-like coordinate vector fields
\partial_{u}
,
\partial_{\underline{u}}
, as there is no danger of confusion. See Section
1.6
for the definitions of regions
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{II},\mathbf{III}
.

Constant
\underline{w}
and
w
spacelike hypersurfaces.
We will use two families of hypersurfaces defined as the level sets of the scalar functions

\underline{w}:={\underline{u}}-r+r_{+},\quad w:=u-r+r_{-}.

The constant
\underline{w}
and
w
hypersurfaces are spacelike, unlike the constant
{\underline{u}},u
hypersurfaces
12
12

12

We have

\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(\nabla{\underline{u}},\nabla{\underline{u}})=\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(\nabla u,\nabla u)=\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}.

. Indeed, see
[
24
]
, we have

\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(\nabla\underline{w},\nabla\underline{w})=\mathbf{g}_{a,M}(\nabla w,\nabla w)=-\frac{r^{2}+2Mr+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}<-1.

Notice that in a region with
{\underline{u}}
large enough, we have
\underline{w}\sim{\underline{u}}.
Recall that in the Kerr black hole interior, the hypersurfaces
\{t=t_{0}\}
are timelike and the hypersurfaces
\{r=r_{0}\}
are spacelike for
r_{0}\in(r_{-},r_{+})
and null for
r_{0}=r_{\pm}
.

Figure 3:
Examples of constant
u,{\underline{u}},w,\underline{w}
hypersurfaces and their causal nature.

2.3
Spin weighted scalars and mode projection

2.3.1
Spin-weighted scalars and spin-weighted spherical operators.

In this section, we consider the round sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
equipped with its volume element, in coordinates
(\theta,\phi)\in(0,\pi)\times[0,2\pi)
:

\mathrm{d}\nu:=\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi.

Let
s
be an integer. Note that in this work we will only consider
s=-2
. A spin
s
scalar is a scalar function that has zero boost weight and proper spin weight, as defined by Geroch, Held and Penrose
[
12
]
. Roughly speaking, a spin
-2
scalar is a the contraction
\alpha(\overline{\eta},\overline{\eta})
of a symmetric tensor
\alpha
with
(\overline{\eta},\overline{\eta})
where
\eta=\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\phi}
. See
[
37
]
for a rigorous presentation of spin-weighted scalars spaces in the Kerr interior, and
[
27
]
for a precise review of the geometric background of the Teukolsky equation.

We define the topological spheres of Kerr spacetime by:

S(u,{\underline{u}}):=\{r=r(u,{\underline{u}})\}\cap\{t=t(u,{\underline{u}})\}.

The volume element induced on
S(u,{\underline{u}})
by the metric
\mathbf{g}_{a,M}
is

\sqrt{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\Delta}\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi

where
\sqrt{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\Delta}\sim 1
in the Kerr black hole interior. Thus, although they are not round, we still use the round volume element
\mathrm{d}\nu
on the Kerr spheres
S(u,{\underline{u}})
to define
L^{2}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))
norms.

Definition 2.1
.

For
\psi
a spin-weighted scalar in the Kerr black hole interior, we define

\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))}:=\left(\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}|\psi^{2}|\mathrm{d}\nu\right)^{1/2}.

Notice that in the ingoing double null-like coordinates, as
\phi_{+}-\phi=r_{mod}
is constant on
S(u,{\underline{u}})
, the definition of the
L^{2}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))
norm gives:

\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))}^{2}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\psi|^{2}(u,{\underline{u}},\theta,\phi_{+})\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi_{+}.

For the same reason, in outgoing double null-like coordinates we also have:

\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))}^{2}=\int_{0}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\psi|^{2}(u,{\underline{u}},\theta,\phi_{-})\sin\theta\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\phi_{-}.

Definition 2.2
.

We recall the definition of the following standard spin-weighted differential operators, called the spherical eth operators:

\displaystyle\mathring{\eth}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\cos\theta}\partial_{\phi}-s\cot\theta,\quad\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}:=\partial_{\theta}-\frac{i}{\cos\theta}\partial_{\phi}+s\cot\theta.

(2.6)

A classical fact is that the spherical eth operators modify the spin when applied to a spin-weighted scalar. More precisely,
\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}
decreases the spin by
1
while
\mathring{\eth}
increases the spin by
1
.

Definition 2.3
.

We define the spin-weighted Laplacian as

\displaystyle\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{2is\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\phi}-(s^{2}\cot^{2}\theta+s).

(2.7)

Remark 2.4
.

We also have the following identity

\displaystyle\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\phi}^{2}+\frac{2is\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{\phi}-(s^{2}\cot^{2}\theta-s)=\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}+2s.

(2.8)

2.3.2
Spin-weighted spherical harmonics.

Let
s
be a fixed spin. The spin-weighted spherical harmonics are the eigenfunctions of the spin-weighted Laplacian, which is self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
. They are given by the following family:

Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi},\quad\ell\geq|s|,-\ell\leq m\leq\ell

of spin
s
scalars on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, and form a complete orthonormal basis of the space of spin
s
scalars on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. When considering the spheres of Kerr interior, as the B-L coordinate
\phi
is singular at the horizons, we need a slightly modified family. Let
r_{0}\in(r_{-},r_{+})
. Then for
(u,{\underline{u}})
such that
r(u,{\underline{u}})\in[r_{0},r_{+}]
, the family

Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}},\quad\ell\geq|s|,-\ell\leq m\leq\ell

is a complete orthonormal basis of the space of spin
s
scalars on
S(u,{\underline{u}})
. Similarly, if
r(u,{\underline{u}})\in[r_{-},r_{0}]
, the family

Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}},\quad\ell\geq|s|,-\ell\leq m\leq\ell

form a complete orthonormal basis of the space of spin
s
scalars on
S(u,{\underline{u}})
. We recall the following standard facts:

\displaystyle\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}(Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}})

\displaystyle=-(\ell-s)(\ell+s+1)Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}(Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}})

\displaystyle=-(\ell+s)(\ell-s+1)Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathring{\eth}(Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}})

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{(\ell-s)(\ell+s+1)}Y_{m,\ell}^{s+1}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}},

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}(Y_{m,\ell}^{s}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(\ell+s)(\ell-s+1)}Y_{m,\ell}^{s-1}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}}.

(2.12)

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\psi
be a spin
-2
scalar. We define

(\psi)^{\pm}_{m,\ell}:=\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}\psi\cdot\overline{Y_{m,\ell}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}}}\mathrm{d}\nu.

Definition 2.6
.

We denote by
\mathbf{P}_{\ell}

the orthogonal projection on the
\ell
mode for spin
-2
scalars. It is the linear operator defined by:

\mathbf{P}_{\ell}(\psi)=\sum_{|m|\leq\ell}(\psi)^{\pm}_{m,\ell}Y_{m,\ell}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{\pm}}.

Note that this definition does not depend on the chosen sign
\pm
. Next, for
\ell^{\prime}\geq 2
we define

\mathbf{P}_{\ell\geq\ell^{\prime}}:=\sum_{\ell\geq\ell^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}_{\ell}=\mathrm{Id}-\sum_{2\leq\ell<\ell^{\prime}}\mathbf{P}_{\ell}.

We will also use the notations

(\psi)_{\ell}=\mathbf{P}_{\ell}(\psi),\quad(\psi)_{\ell\geq\ell^{\prime}}=\mathbf{P}_{\ell\geq\ell^{\prime}}(\psi).

The following result is a standard fact, and is responsible for the mode coupling that appears when projecting the Teukolsky equation onto
\ell
modes in Kerr spacetimes.

Proposition 2.7
.

There are constants
\{c_{m,\ell}^{-2}\}_{|m|\leq\ell}
and
\{b_{m,\ell}^{-2}\}_{|m|\leq\ell}
such that for any spin
{-2}
scalar
\psi
and any
\ell\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle(\cos\theta\psi)^{\pm}_{m,\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell-1}^{\ell+1}b_{m,\ell^{\prime}}^{-2}\psi^{\pm}_{m,\ell^{\prime}},

(2.13)

\displaystyle(\sin^{2}\theta\psi)^{\pm}_{m,\ell}=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=\ell-2}^{\ell+2}c_{m,\ell^{\prime}}^{-2}\psi^{\pm}_{m,\ell^{\prime}}.

(2.14)

Proof.

See for example
[
25
, Prop. 2.13]
.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

We define the following spin-weighted operator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:=\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\Phi+a\sin\theta T+is\cot\theta\frac{\Sigma}{r^{2}+a^{2}}.

(2.15)

This operator
\mathcal{U}
is useful to write a Poincaré-type inequality in Kerr, see
[
15
]
. For a spin-weighted scalar
\psi
we have
\mathcal{U}\psi\in L^{\infty}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))
(see for example
[
37
, (2.34)]
). The following integration by parts lemma is
[
15
, Lem. 2.10]
:

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\psi,\varphi
be spin
s
scalars. Then

\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}\overline{\varphi}\>\mathcal{U}\psi\mathrm{d}\nu=T\left(\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}a\sin\theta\overline{\varphi}\psi\mathrm{d}\nu\right)-\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}\overline{\mathcal{U}\varphi}\psi\mathrm{d}\nu.

We can express the Killing vector fields
T
and
\Phi
with
e_{3}
,
e_{4}
and
\mathcal{U}
in the following way:

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Sigma}(\mu e_{3}+e_{4})-\frac{a\sin\theta}{\Sigma}\mathcal{U}+\frac{ias\cos\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}},

(2.16)

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin\theta(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Sigma}(\mathcal{U}-a\sin\theta e_{4}-a\sin\theta\mu e_{3})-is\cos\theta.

(2.17)

In particular,
T=O(\mu)e_{3}+O(1)e_{4}+O(\sin\theta)\mathcal{U}+O(1)
, and
\Phi=O(\mu)e_{3}+O(1)e_{4}+O(\sin\theta)\mathcal{U}+O(1)
.

2.4
Analytic preliminaries

Definition 2.10
.

We define the spin-weighted Carter operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{s}:=a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta T^{2}-2ias\cos\theta T+\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}.

(2.18)

This operator is crucial in our analysis because it commutes with the Teukolsky operator (see (
2.24
)), and thus will help control angular derivatives of solutions of the Teukolsky equation. We now state a Sobolev inequality on
S(u,{\underline{u}})
for spin-weighted scalars. It will be used throughout the paper to deduce pointwise decay from
L^{2}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))
decay of Carter derivatives and
T
derivatives.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\psi
be a spin
\pm 2
scalar. For any
(u,{\underline{u}})
we have

\displaystyle\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))}{\lesssim}\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}\left(|T^{\leq 2}\psi|^{2}+|\mathcal{Q}_{s}\psi|^{2}\right)\>\mathrm{d}\nu.

(2.19)

Proof.

The proof is a simple re-writing of the standard Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, see
[
15
, Prop. 2.16]
.
∎

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
\psi
be a spin
-2
scalar. We have for
\ell\geq 2
,
|m|\leq\ell
,

|(\psi)_{m,\ell}^{\pm}|\lesssim\ell^{-2}\|\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{\leq 1}T^{\leq 2}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))}.

Proof.

We use the following identity, which is deduced from (
2.9
), and the fact that
\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}
is self-adjoint on the sphere:

(\psi)_{m,\ell}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{(\ell+2)(\ell-1)}(\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}\psi)_{m,\ell}^{\pm},

and all is left to do is to replace the spin-weighted Laplacian
\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}
with the Carter operator and
T
derivatives using (
2.18
).
∎

Next, we state a Poincaré-type inequality which will be used to absorb the
0
order terms in the energy estimate for Teukolsky.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\psi
be a spin weighted scalar. We define respectively the energy density and degenerate energy density of
\psi
as

\mathbf{e}[\psi]:=|e_{3}\psi|^{2}+|e_{4}\psi|^{2}+|\mathcal{U}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}\psi|^{2},

\mathbf{e}_{deg}[\psi]:=\mu^{2}|e_{3}\psi|^{2}+|e_{4}\psi|^{2}+|\mathcal{U}\psi|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}\psi|^{2}.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
\psi
be a spin
-2
scalar. For any
(u,{\underline{u}})
we have the Poincaré inequality

\displaystyle\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))}{\lesssim}\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}\mathbf{e}_{deg}[\psi]\mathrm{d}\nu.

(2.20)

Proof.

The proof is a simple re-writing of the standard Poincaré inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, see
[
15
, Prop. 2.14]
.
∎

The following result is used to deduce decay of the energy from an energy estimate.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
p>1
and
0\leq\gamma<1
and let
f:[1,+\infty)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
be a continuous function. Assume that there are constants
C_{0}>0,C_{1}>0,C_{2}\geq 0
,
C_{3}\geq 0
such that for
1\leq x_{1}<x_{2}
,

f(x_{2})+C_{1}\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}x^{-\gamma}f(x)\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{0}f(x_{1})+C_{2}\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}x^{-p}\mathrm{~d}x+C_{3}x_{1}^{-p}.

Then for any
x_{1}\geq 1
,

f(x_{1})\leq Cx_{1}^{-p+\gamma}

where
C
is a constant that depends only on
f(1),C_{0},C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},p
, and
\gamma
.

Proof.

See
[
24
, Lemma 3.4]
.
∎

2.5
The Teukolsky equations

2.5.1
Definition and expressions of the Teukolsky operators

We define the Teukolsky operator
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}
as the operator on the LHS of the Teukolsky equation (
1.5
), namely

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}:=-

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\left(r^{2}+a^{2}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right]\partial_{t}^{2}-\frac{4Mar}{\Delta}\partial_{t}\partial_{\phi}-\left[\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right]\partial_{\phi}^{2}

\displaystyle+\Delta^{-s}\partial_{r}\left(\Delta^{s+1}\partial_{r}\right)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}\left(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\right)+2s\left[\frac{a(r-M)}{\Delta}+\frac{i\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right]\partial_{\phi}

\displaystyle+2s\left[\frac{M\left(r^{2}-a^{2}\right)}{\Delta}-r-ia\cos\theta\right]\partial_{t}-\left[\frac{s^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}-s\right].

We also define the rescaled Teukolsky operator
\mathbf{T}_{s}
by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}:=\Delta^{s}\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}(\Delta^{-s}\cdot).

(2.21)

In the rest of the paper,
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
satisfies the Teukolsky equation
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}\widehat{\psi}_{-2}=0
. Thus, denoting

\psi_{-2}:=\Delta^{-2}\widehat{\psi}_{-2},

the Teukolsky equation rewrites
\mathbf{T}_{-2}\psi_{-2}=0
, where the expression of
\mathbf{T}_{s}
in B-L coordinates is:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}=-

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\left(r^{2}+a^{2}\right)^{2}}{\Delta}-a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta\right]T^{2}-\frac{4Mar}{\Delta}T\Phi-\left[\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right]\Phi^{2}

\displaystyle+\Delta^{-s}\partial_{r}\left(\Delta^{s+1}\partial_{r}\right)+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}\left(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta}\right)+2s\left[\frac{a(r-M)}{\Delta}+\frac{i\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\right]\Phi

\displaystyle+2s\left[\frac{M\left(r^{2}-a^{2}\right)}{\Delta}-r-ia\cos\theta\right]T-\left[\frac{s^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}+s\right]-4s(r-M)\partial_{r}.

The Teukolsky operators can also be written as, see for example
[
25
, (3.3)]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}=-\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\Delta\sin^{2}\theta}{\Delta}T^{2}+\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r})-\frac{4aMr}{\Delta}T\Phi-\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}\Phi^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}-2ias\cos\theta T-2s[(r-M)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+2rT],

(2.22)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}=-\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\Delta\sin^{2}\theta}{\Delta}T^{2}+\partial_{r}(\Delta\partial_{r})-\frac{4aMr}{\Delta}T\Phi-\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}\Phi^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}-2ias\cos\theta T+2s[(r-M)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}-2rT].

(2.23)

These expressions directly prove that
T
,
\Phi
, and the Carter operator
\mathcal{Q}_{s}
commute with the Teukolsky equation, i.e.

\displaystyle[T,\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}]=[T,\mathbf{T}_{s}]=[\mathcal{Q}_{s},\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}]=[\mathcal{Q}_{s},\mathbf{T}_{s}]=[\Phi,\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}]=[\Phi,\mathbf{T}_{s}]=0.

(2.24)

The following expressions of the Teukolsky operators will be convenient in the energy estimates:

Proposition 2.16
.

We have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}=

\displaystyle-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}+\mathcal{U}^{2}+\dfrac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})-4ias\cos\theta T+2is\dfrac{a^{2}\sin\theta\cos\theta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})}\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle+2r(e_{4}-\mu e_{3})+4s[(r-M)e_{3}-rT]+\dfrac{2ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi-s-s^{2}\dfrac{a^{4}\sin^{2}\theta\cos^{2}\theta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}},

(2.25)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}=

\displaystyle-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}+\mathcal{U}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})-4ias\cos\theta T+2is\frac{a^{2}\sin\theta\cos\theta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})}\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle+2r(e_{4}-\mu e_{3})+4s[(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}-rT]+\frac{2ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi+s-s^{2}\frac{a^{4}\sin^{2}\theta\cos^{2}\theta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}.

(2.26)

Proof.

See
[
15
, Prop. 2.17]
.
∎

The spin
+2
equivalent of the following identity was crucial in
[
15
]
.

Proposition 2.17
.

For any spin
-2
scalar
\widehat{\psi}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{e_{3}}((r^{2}+a^{2})\Delta^{-2}e_{4}\widehat{\psi})=\frac{1}{4}\mu\Delta^{-2}\left[\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}-\mathcal{Q}_{-2}-2aT\Phi-6rT\right]\widehat{\psi}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\widehat{e_{3}}((r^{2}+a^{2})\Delta^{-2}e_{4}\widehat{\psi})

\displaystyle=r\mu\Delta^{-2}e_{4}\widehat{\psi}-2\Delta^{-2}(r-M)e_{4}\widehat{\psi}+(r^{2}+a^{2})\Delta^{-2}\widehat{e_{3}}e_{4}\widehat{\psi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\mu\Delta^{-2}(-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}-8(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}+4re_{4})\widehat{\psi}.

Using
e_{4}-\mu e_{3}=\mu\partial_{r}=2e_{4}-T-a/(r^{2}+a^{2})\Phi
we get

\displaystyle\widehat{e_{3}}((r^{2}+a^{2})

\displaystyle\Delta^{-2}e_{4}\widehat{\psi})=

(2.27)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\mu\Delta^{-2}\Big(-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}-8(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}+2r(e_{4}-\mu e_{3})+2rT+\frac{2ra}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi\Big)\widehat{\psi}.

Next, using (
2.26
) we get

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}=

\displaystyle-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}+\widetilde{U}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})+8ia\cos\theta T

\displaystyle+2r(e_{4}-\mu e_{3})-8[(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}-rT]+\frac{2ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi-2,

where

\widetilde{U}^{2}:=\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\theta}\Phi^{2}+a^{2}\sin^{2}T^{2}+2aT\Phi-4ai\cos\theta T-\frac{4i\cos\theta}{\sin^{2}\theta}\Phi-4\cot^{2}\theta.

We infer, using the definition of the Carter operator (
2.18
),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}=

\displaystyle-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}+2r(e_{4}-\mu e_{3})-8(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}+\frac{2ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}+a^{2}\sin^{2}T^{2}+2aT\Phi+4ai\cos\theta T+8rT

\displaystyle=-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}+2r(e_{4}-\mu e_{3})-8(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}+\frac{2ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathcal{Q}_{-2}+2aT\Phi+8rT.

Combining this identity with (
2.27
) concludes the proof.
∎

2.5.2
Decomposition
(\partial_{t},\partial_{r})
of the Teukolsky operators

The following
(\partial_{t},\partial_{r})
decompositions of the Teukolsky operators will be crucial in this paper. We will use them to reduce the Teukolsky equation to an ODE along hypersurfaces of constant
u
and
{\underline{u}}
.

Proposition 2.18
.

We have the decomposition of the Teukolsky operator

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}=\mathbf{T}_{s}^{[\partial_{r}]}+\mathbf{T}_{s}^{[\partial_{t}]},

(2.28)

where, recalling the definitions of the coordinate vector fields
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}
,
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}
in Section
2.2.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}^{[\partial_{r}]}

\displaystyle:=\Delta\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^{2}+2a\Phi\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}-2(r-M)(s-1)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime},

(2.29)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}^{[\partial_{t}]}

\displaystyle:=a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta T^{2}+2(r^{2}+a^{2})T\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+2aT\Phi+(2r(1-2s)-2ias\cos\theta)T.

(2.30)

Similarly we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}=\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{[\partial_{r}]}+\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{[\partial_{t}]},

(2.31)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{[\partial_{r}]}

\displaystyle:=\Delta\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}^{2}-2a\Phi\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}+2(r-M)(s+1)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}+\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth},

(2.32)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{[\partial_{t}]}

\displaystyle:=a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta T^{2}-2(r^{2}+a^{2})T\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}+2aT\Phi-(2r(1+2s)+2ias\cos\theta)T.

(2.33)

Proof.

Using (
2.22
) and

\partial_{r}=\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\Phi+\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}T,

we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{s}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}-a^{2}\Delta\sin^{2}\theta}{\Delta}T^{2}+\left(\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\Phi+\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}T\right)(\Delta\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+a\Phi+(r^{2}+a^{2})T)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad-\frac{4aMr}{\Delta}T\Phi-\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}\Phi^{2}+\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}-2ias\cos\theta T-2s[(r-M)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+2rT]

\displaystyle=\Delta\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^{2}+a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta T^{2}+2(r-M)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+2a\Phi\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+2(r^{2}+a^{2})T\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}\Phi^{2}+\frac{2a(r^{2}+a^{2})}{\Delta}T\Phi

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+2rT-\frac{4aMr}{\Delta}T\Phi-\frac{a^{2}}{\Delta}\Phi^{2}+\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}-2ias\cos\theta T-2s[(r-M)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}+2rT]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{T}_{s}^{[\partial_{r}]}+\mathbf{T}_{s}^{[\partial_{t}]},

as stated in (
2.28
). The proof of (
2.31
) is similar.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

We have the commutator identities

[\mathbf{T}_{s},\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}]=[4(r-M)e_{3}-4rT+2(s-1)]\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}-2(1-2s)T,

[\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s},\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}]=-[4(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}-4rT+2(s+1)]\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}+2(1+2s)T.

Proof.

We simply use the above expressions (
2.30
), (
2.29
), (
2.32
), (
2.33
), as well as
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}=-2e_{3}
and
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}=2\mu^{-1}e_{4}
.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

We have, for any
\ell\geq 2
:

[\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbf{P}_{\ell}]=[\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}},\mathbf{P}_{\ell}]=[\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{r}]},\mathbf{P}_{\ell}]=[\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}^{[\partial_{r}]},\mathbf{P}_{\ell}]=0.

Proof.

Using the two expressions

\mathbf{P}_{\ell}(\psi)=\sum_{|m|\leq\ell}(\psi)^{+}_{m,\ell}Y_{m,\ell}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}=\sum_{|m|\leq\ell}(\psi)^{-}_{m,\ell}Y_{m,\ell}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}},

as well as
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}(Y_{m,\ell}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}})=\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}(Y_{m,\ell}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}})=0
, we deduce respectively
[\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}},\mathbf{P}_{\ell}]=0
and
[\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}},\mathbf{P}_{\ell}]=0
. The two remaining identities come from (
2.29
) and (
2.32
), where we also use the fact that
\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}
and
\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\mathring{\eth}
are self-adjoint on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
∎

2.6
Subregions of the Kerr black hole interior

We will divide the Kerr black hole interior
\mathbb{R}_{\underline{u}}\times\mathbb{R}_{u}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
into different regions. We fix
r_{\mathfrak{b}}>r_{-}
close to
r_{-}
and
\gamma>0
small. The final values of
\gamma
and
r_{\mathfrak{b}}
will be chosen in the energy estimates, respectively in Section
4.1
and in Appendix
A.2
. We define the hypersurface
13
13

13

The hypersurface
\Gamma
is spacelike for
r
is sufficiently close to
r_{-}
.

\Gamma:=\{2r^{*}={\underline{u}}^{\gamma}\}\cap\{{\underline{u}}\geq 1\},

and the following subregions of the Kerr black hole interior:

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}:=\{r_{\mathfrak{b}}\leq r\leq r_{+}\}\cap\{{\underline{u}}\geq 1\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{II}:=\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{2r^{*}\leq{\underline{u}}^{\gamma}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{III}:=\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{2r^{*}\geq{\underline{u}}^{\gamma}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\},

where
w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}:=2r_{\mathfrak{b}}^{*}-(2r_{\mathfrak{b}}^{*})^{1/\gamma}-r_{\mathfrak{b}}+r_{-}
is such that
\{w=w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\}
intersects
\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}
at
\Gamma\cap\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}
(recall the definition of
w
in Section
2.2.2
). See Figure
4
for an illustration of regions
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{II}
,
\mathbf{III}
.

Figure 4:
Subregions
\mathbf{I},\mathbf{II}
and
\mathbf{III}
of the Kerr black hole interior.

Region
\mathbf{I}
is the redshift region close to
\mathcal{H_{+}}
, region
\mathbf{III}
is the blueshift region close to
\mathcal{CH_{+}}
and region
\mathbf{II}
is an intermediate region where the blueshift effect is already present. Notice that with our choice of
r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma
, we get that
w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\ll-1
is negative and large in absolute value. This implies
u\ll-1
in
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
.

Lemma 2.21
.

We have
|u|\sim{\underline{u}}
in
\mathbf{II}
and
{\underline{u}}\geq|u|
in
\mathbf{III}
.

Proof.

The first point comes from the definition of
\mathbf{II}
:
u+{\underline{u}}=2r^{*}\leq{\underline{u}}^{\gamma}
which implies
{\underline{u}}\leq|u|+{\underline{u}}^{\gamma}
thus
{\underline{u}}\lesssim|u|
for
{\underline{u}}\geq 1
large enough. Moreover in
\mathbf{II}
we also have
2r^{*}\geq 2r_{\mathfrak{b}}^{*}
which implies
|u|=-u\leq{\underline{u}}-2r_{\mathfrak{b}}^{*}\leq{\underline{u}}
, where we use
r^{*}\geq 0
for
r
close to
r_{-}
. The second fact comes from
|u|=-u={\underline{u}}-2r^{*}\leq{\underline{u}}
in
\mathbf{III}
.
∎

3
Statement of the main theorem

3.1
Assumptions on the event horizon

Our main result will apply to a spin
-2
scalar solution of the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation, which is initially smooth and compactly supported on a spacelike hypersurface as in
[
15
,
25
]
. In practice, we will only rely on Price’s law results on the event horizon proven in
[
25
]
which hold under such assumptions and which we recall below.

We define the set of derivatives which are tangential to the event horizon:

\overline{\nabla}:=\{\Phi,T,\mathring{\eth},\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}\}.

For a multi-index
k=(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, we define
|k|:=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4}
, and

\overline{\nabla}^{k}=\Phi^{k_{1}}T^{k_{2}}\mathring{\eth}^{k_{3}}(\mathring{\eth}^{\prime})^{k_{4}}.

Let
N_{j}
,
N_{k}
be sufficiently large integers that we will choose later, see Remark
3.4
. We will consider a spin
-2
scalar
\psi_{-2}
solution of the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation

\mathbf{T}_{-2}\psi_{-2}=0

such that there are constants
Q_{m,2}
for
|m|\leq 2
,
\delta>0
small, such that for
j\leq N_{j}
and
|k|\leq N_{k}

\displaystyle\left|e_{3}^{\leq 1}T^{j}\overline{\nabla}^{k}\left(\psi_{-2}-\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)\right|\lesssim{\underline{u}}^{-7-j-\delta},\quad\text{on}\>\mathcal{H_{+}}\cap\{{\underline{u}}\geq 1\}.

(3.1)

Remark 3.1
.

Price’s law type assumption (
3.1
) was proven in
[
25
]
by Ma and Zhang
14
14

14

The decay of the tangential derivatives
\overline{\nabla}^{k}
is not explicitely stated in
[
25
]
, but can be easily deduced from the fact that
\Phi
,
T
and the Carter operator commute with the Teukolsky equation, and by applying the main theorem of
[
25
]
. The statement for the
e_{3}
derivative comes from
[
25
, (4.101)]
, recalling
e_{3}({\underline{u}})=e_{3}(\theta)=e_{3}(\phi_{+})=0
.
for solutions of the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation which are initially smooth and compactly supported on a spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
defined in
[
25
, Section 2.1]
, where
Q_{m,2}=2^{7}\mathbb{Q}_{m,2}/5
with
\mathbb{Q}_{m,2}
defined in
[
25
, Lem. 5.7]
. Their proof holds for
|a|\ll M
, and for
|a|<M
conditionally on an energy-Morawetz estimate for the Teukolsky equation in subextremal Kerr. This estimate was then proven in the whole subextremal range
|a|<M
in
[
5
,
6
]
.

Using the results in
[
15
]
for the spin
-2
equation in the redshift region
\mathbf{I}
, in the present article we will be able to restrict most of the analysis to regions
\mathbf{II}
and
\mathbf{III}
. The first example of this is the following result, which follows from
[
15
, Theo. 4.11]
:

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume that
\psi_{-2}
is a solution of the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation in the Kerr black hole interior for
0<|a|<M
that satisfies assumption (
3.1
). Then,

\psi_{-2}=\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}+\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}],

where for
j\leq N_{j}-2
and
2k_{1}+k_{2}\leq N_{k}-3
, we have in
\mathbf{I}
,

\displaystyle|\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^{\leq 1}T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]|\lesssim{\underline{u}}^{-7-j-\delta},

(3.2)

and, recalling the definition of the energy density in Definition
2.13
, the energy bound on
\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}
,

\displaystyle\iint_{\left\{\underline{w}_{1}\leq\underline{w}\leq w_{2}\right\}\cap\left\{r=r_{b}\right\}}\mathbf{e}\left[T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^{\leq 1}\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]\right]\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\underline{u}\lesssim\underline{w}_{1}^{-2(7+\delta)-2j}+\int_{\underline{w}_{1}}^{\underline{w}_{2}}\underline{w}^{-2(7+\delta)-2j}\mathrm{~d}\underline{w}.

(3.3)

Proof.

We prove that
\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]
satisfies the assumptions of
[
15
, Theo. 4.11]
with
\beta=7+\delta
. Assumption
[
15
, (4.18)]
is (
3.1
). Now we check
[
15
, (4.19)]
. Using the decomposition (
2.28
) of the Teukolsky operator, we have the explicit computation

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^{\leq 1}T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\mathbf{T}_{-2}\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^{\leq 1}T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\mathbf{T}_{-2}\left(\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^{\leq 1}T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\mathbf{T}_{-2}^{[\partial_{t}]}\left(\frac{1}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim{\underline{u}}^{-8-j},

(3.4)

where we used (
2.30
) as well as
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}{\underline{u}}=\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}\phi_{+}=\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}\theta=\mathring{\eth}\mathring{\eth}^{\prime}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)=0
by (
2.10
) for the before-to-last step. We conclude the proof by applying
[
15
, Theo. 4.11]
.
∎

3.2
Precise version of the main theorem

The following theorem is the main result of this paper.

Theorem 3.3

(Main theorem, precise version)
.

Assume that
\psi_{-2}
is a smooth spin
-2
scalar which satisfies the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation
\mathbf{T}_{-2}\psi_{-2}=0
in the Kerr black hole interior with
0<|a|<M
and such that (
3.1
) holds on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}_{+}
. Then denoting
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}=\Delta^{2}\psi_{-2}
, we have in region
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}=\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\}
the following asymptotic behavior:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{-2}(u,r,\theta,\phi_{-})=\frac{1}{u^{8}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}C_{m}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}+O(|u|^{-8-\delta/2})+O(r-r_{-}),

(3.5)

where, recalling the definition of the constants
b_{m,\ell}^{-2}
in Proposition
2.7
,

\displaystyle C_{m}=\frac{7Q_{m,2}}{2(r_{-}-M)+iam}\int_{r_{-}}^{r_{+}}\Delta^{2}(r^{\prime})e^{2imr_{mod}(r^{\prime})}(5r^{\prime}+iam+2iab_{m,2}^{-2})\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}.

(3.6)

Remark 3.4
.

We remark the following:

1.

As explained in Section
3.1
, Theorem
3.3
can be applied to a solution
\psi_{-2}
which is initially smooth and compactly supported on a spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
as in
[
25
]
. In particular, we do not need to assume higher order decay of the higher modes
(\psi_{-2})_{\ell\geq 3}
on
\mathcal{H}_{+}
.

2.

The constants
C_{m}
are generically non-zero. Indeed, for generic initial data,
Q_{m,2}\neq 0
(see
[
25
, Lem. 5.7]
), and except for isolated values of black hole parameters
(a,M)
,

\int_{r_{-}}^{r_{+}}\Delta^{2}(r^{\prime})e^{2imr_{mod}(r^{\prime})}(5r^{\prime}+iam+2iab_{m,2}^{-2})\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\neq 0.

This can be seen most easily for
m=0
, as the real part of the above integral is positive.

3.

The asymptotic behavior (
3.5
) implies that for a large enough constant
C>0
, on
\mathcal{CH_{+}}\cap\{u\leq-C\}
we have generically

\widehat{\psi}_{-2}\sim\frac{1}{u^{8}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}C_{m}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}\neq 0.

Together with the discussion in Section
1.3
, this result suggests that gravitational perturbations of a Kerr black hole build up to form a coordinate-independent curvature singularity at the perturbed Cauchy horizon.

4.

Inspecting the proof, we find that (
3.5
) holds choosing
N_{k}\geq 12
and
N_{j}\geq 10
, see Remark
5.17
. We did not try to optimize the loss of derivatives. Also assuming that (
3.1
) holds for a higher number of
T
and
\overline{\nabla}
derivatives implies that the asymptotic behavior (
3.5
) holds for a corresponding higher number of
T
,
\mathcal{Q}_{-2}
and
\Phi
derivatives.

5.

The asymptotic behavior (
3.5
) confirms the heuristic prediction by Ori in
[
30
]
.

3.3
Proof of the main theorem

Theorem
3.3
is a consequence of the three following results, for a smooth spin
-2
scalar
\psi_{-2}
such that
\mathbf{T}_{-2}\psi_{-2}=0
in the Kerr black hole interior with
0<|a|<M
, with
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}=\Delta^{2}\psi_{-2}
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Assume that
\psi_{-2}
satisfies (
3.1
). Then we have in
\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\}
:

(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell=2}=\frac{1}{u^{8}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}C_{m}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{-}}+O(|u|^{-8-\delta/2})+O(r-r_{-}),

where the constants
C_{m}
are defined by (
3.6
).

Theorem 3.6
.

Assume that
\psi_{-2}
satisfies (
3.1
). Then we have in region
\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\}
,

|(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell=3}|\lesssim|u|^{-8-\delta/2}+r-r_{-}.

Theorem 3.7
.

Assume that
\psi_{-2}
satisfies (
3.1
). Then we have in
\{r_{-}\leq r\leq r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}\cap\{w\leq w_{r_{\mathfrak{b}},\gamma}\}
,

|(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell\geq 4}|\lesssim|u|^{-8-\delta/2}+r-r_{-}.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
3.3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

We decompose
\widehat{\psi}_{-2}
as follows:

\widehat{\psi}_{-2}=(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell=2}+(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell=3}+(\widehat{\psi}_{-2})_{\ell\geq 4}.

The proof of Theorem
3.3
then proceeds by combining Theorems
3.5
,
3.6
, and
3.7
.
∎

The rest of the paper is devoted to the proofs of Theorems
3.5
,
3.6
, and
3.7
. In Section
4
, we prove energy estimates and pointwise bounds for the spin
-2
Teukolsky equation in the whole Kerr black hole interior. These bounds are then used in Section
5.1
and
5.2
where we prove Theorem
3.5
, in Sections
5.3
and
5.4
where we prove Theorem
3.6
, and finally in Sections
5.5
and
5.6
where we prove Theorem
3.7
.

4
Upper bound for
\mathrm{Err}[\widehat{\psi}_{-2}]
near
\mathcal{CH_{+}}

From now on,
\psi_{-2}
satisfies
\mathbf{T}_{-2}\psi_{-2}=0
, and we denote:

\mathrm{Err}[\widehat{\psi}_{-2}]=\Delta^{2}\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]=\widehat{\psi}_{-2}-\frac{\Delta^{2}}{{\underline{u}}^{7}}\sum_{|m|\leq 2}Q_{m,2}Y_{m,2}^{-2}(\cos\theta)e^{im\phi_{+}}.

4.1
Energy method for
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}
in region
\mathbf{II}

We begin this section by introducing a modified Teukolsky operator
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}^{(c,V)}
. Similarly as in
[
15
]
, this definition will be used to unify the treatment of the PDEs satisfied by
\mathrm{Err}[\widehat{\psi}_{-2}]
and
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}\mathrm{Err}[\widehat{\psi}_{-2}]
after commuting the Teukolsky equation with
\partial_{r_{\mathrm{out}}}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
V,c
be real numbers. We define the following spin-weighted operator

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{(c,V)}:=

\displaystyle-4(r^{2}+a^{2})e_{3}e_{4}+\mathcal{U}^{2}+\frac{1}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\theta}(\sin\theta\partial_{\theta})-4ias\cos\theta T+2is\frac{a^{2}\sin\theta\cos\theta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})}\mathcal{U}

(4.1)

\displaystyle+2r(e_{4}-\mu e_{3})+4cs[(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}-rT]+\frac{2ar}{r^{2}+a^{2}}\Phi+s-s^{2}\frac{a^{4}\sin^{2}\theta\cos^{2}\theta}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{2}}+V.

Remark 4.2
.

We remark the following identities, that are deduced from (
2.26
) and (
4.1
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{(c,V)}=\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}+4s(c-1)[(r-M)\mu^{-1}e_{4}-rT]+V,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}=\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{(1,0)}.

(4.3)

In this paper, we will only use a finite number of constants
c,V
, so that we do not make explicit the dependance in
c,V
in the notations
\lesssim
and
O
. Notice that (
4.2
) above implies, together with (
2.24
),

\displaystyle[\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{(c,V)},T]=[\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{(c,V)},\Phi]=[\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{s}^{(c,V)},\mathcal{Q}_{s}]=0.

(4.4)

In this section, we will consider a spin
-2
scalar
\widehat{\psi}
satisfying, for
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
c>0
,
V\in\mathbb{R}
and
0\leq j\leq N_{j}^{\prime}
,
0\leq 2k_{1}+k_{2}\leq N_{k}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\bullet\>

\displaystyle T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}=O({\underline{u}}^{-\beta-j})\text{ on }\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\},

(4.5)

\displaystyle\bullet\>

\displaystyle\iint_{\left\{\underline{w}_{1}\leq\underline{w}\leq\underline{w}_{2}\right\}\cap\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}}\mathbf{e}[T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}]\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\underline{u}\lesssim\underline{w}_{1}^{-2\beta-2j}+\int_{\underline{w}_{1}}^{\underline{w}_{2}}\underline{w}^{-2\beta-2j}\mathrm{d}\underline{w},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\bullet\>

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}^{(c,V)}T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}=O({\underline{u}}^{-\beta-j})\text{ in }\mathbf{II}.

(4.7)

Note that (
4.7
) will be satisfied by
\mathrm{Err}[\psi_{-2}]
with
\beta=7+\delta
, see Proposition
4.9
. The rest of this section is dedicated to proving the almost-propagation of the polynomial decay (
4.5
) from the spacelike hypersurface
\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}
to the whole region
\mathbf{II}
, see Proposition
4.8
. We will follow the strategy of
[
24
]
which introduces a log-degenerate energy control in the interior of the Kerr black hole for the scalar wave equation. This section is an extension of this method to the Teukolsky equation. When following the energy method of
[
24
]
, we will see that the negative
-2
spin for the modified Teukolsky operator
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}_{-2}^{(c,V)}
will give the positivity of the bulk term in the energy estimate. Contrary to what was done in
[
15
]
for the spin
+2
Teukolsky operator
\mathbf{T}_{+2}^{(c,V)}
, we cannot directly control the non-degenerate energy of the solution, but a degenerate one, and there is a loss of precision of order
\gamma
in the final polynomial bound.

Definition 4.3
.

For
\widehat{\psi}
a spin-weighted scalar, and
\alpha\geq 0
, we define

\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}[\widehat{\psi}]:=(-\mu)|{\log(-\mu)}|^{-\alpha}|e_{3}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+|e_{4}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+|\mathcal{U}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}.

Remark 4.4
.

Let us note the following concerning
\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}
:

•

The weight
(-\mu)|{\log(-\mu)}|^{-\alpha}
degenerates at the event and Cauchy horizons.

•

Although the proof of the main theorem works with any fixed value of
\alpha>1
, in practice we will only use the value
\alpha=3/2
, so we still denote the
\alpha
-dependent bounds with
O
and
\lesssim
. For example,
\mu^{2}\lesssim(-\mu)|{\log(-\mu)}|^{-\alpha}
so
\mathbf{e}_{deg}[\widehat{\psi}]\lesssim\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}[\widehat{\psi}]
in
\mathbf{II}\cup\mathbf{III}
.

We first prove the following energy estimate:

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\widehat{\psi}
be a spin
-2
scalar satisfying (
4.7
) with
\beta,V\in\mathbb{R}
,
c>0
. Then for
p=p(a,M)>0
sufficiently large, and
r_{\mathfrak{b}}=r_{\mathfrak{b}}(a,M)
sufficiently close to
r_{-}
, we have, for
0\leq j\leq N_{j}^{\prime}
,
0\leq 2k_{1}+k_{1}\leq N_{k}^{\prime}
,
\alpha>1
, and
1\leq\underline{w}_{1}\leq\underline{w}_{2}
:

\displaystyle\iint_{\left\{\underline{w}=\underline{w}_{2}\right\}\cap\mathbf{II}}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}[T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}](-\mu)\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}u+\iiint_{\left\{\underline{w}_{1}\leq\underline{w}\leq\underline{w}_{2}\right\}\cap\mathbf{II}}\underline{w}^{-\gamma}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}[T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}](-\mu)\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}\underline{u}

\displaystyle+\iint_{\Gamma\cap\left\{\underline{w}_{1}\leq\underline{w}\leq\underline{w}_{2}\right\}}\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}[T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}]\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\underline{u}\lesssim\iint_{\left\{\underline{w}=\underline{w}_{1}\right\}\cap\mathbf{II}}\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}[T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}](-\mu)\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle+\iint_{\left\{\underline{w}_{1}\leq\underline{w}\leq\underline{w}_{2}\right\}\cap\{r=r_{\mathfrak{b}}\}}\mathbf{e}[T^{j}\mathcal{Q}_{-2}^{k_{1}}\Phi^{k_{2}}\widehat{\psi}]\mathrm{d}\nu\mathrm{d}\underline{u}+\int_{\underline{w}_{1}}^{\underline{w}_{2}}\underline{w}^{-2\beta-2j}\mathrm{d}\underline{u},

where for any
\widehat{\psi}
, denoting
f(r)=|\log(-\mu)|^{-\alpha}
and
g(r)=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{p}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\Gamma}[\widehat{\psi}]=

\displaystyle 2(r^{2}+a^{2})g(r)|\partial_{\underline{u}}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mu f(r)(|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+|\mathcal{U}\widehat{\psi}|^{2})-a\sin\theta\mu\mathfrak{R}\left(\overline{X(\widehat{\psi})}\mathcal{U}\widehat{\psi})\right)

\displaystyle+(1-\gamma{\underline{u}}^{\gamma-1})\left(2(r^{2}+a^{2})f(r)|\widehat{e_{3}}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mu g(r)(|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi}|^{2}+|\mathcal{U}\widehat{\psi}|^{2})+a\sin\theta\mu\mathfrak{R}\left(\overline{X(\widehat{\psi})}\mathcal{U}\widehat{\psi})\right)\right),

and
X(\widehat{\psi})=|{\log(-\mu)}|^{-\alpha}\widehat{e_{3}}\widehat{\psi}+(r^{2}+a^{2})^{p}\partial_{\underline{u}}\widehat{\psi}
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the case
j=k_{1}=k_{2}=0
. We fix the spin
s=-2
but we do the computations for a general spin
s
. Recall that defining
\widehat{e_{3}}=-\mu e_{3}
we have in outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates
\partial_{u}=\widehat{e_{3}}+a/(r^{2}+a^{2})\Phi
and
\partial_{\underline{u}}=e_{4}
. Thus using (
4.1
) we get in
\mathbf{II}
:
